Electric, thermal, and thermoelectric magnetoconductivity for Weyl/multi-Weyl semimetals in planar Hall set-ups induced by the combined effects of topology and strain
Authors: Leonardo Medel, Rahul Ghosh, Alberto Martín-Ruiz, Ipsita Mandal
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
We continue our investigation of the response tensors in planar Hall (or planar thermal Hall) configurations where a three-dimensional Weyl/multi-Weyl semimetal is subjected to the combined influence of an electric field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E (and/or temperature gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _{{\textbf{r}} } T$$\end{document}∇rT) and an effective magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ, generalizing the considerations of Phys. Rev. B 108 (2023) 155132 and Physica E 159 (2024) 115914. The electromagnetic fields are oriented at a generic angle with respect to each other, thus leading to the possibility of having collinear components, which do not arise in a Hall set-up. The net effective magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ consists of two parts—(a) an actual/physical magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B applied externally; and (b) an emergent magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 which quantifies the elastic deformations of the sample. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 is an axial pseudomagnetic field because it couples to conjugate nodal points with opposite chiralities with opposite signs. Using a semiclassical Boltzmann formalism, we derive the generic expressions for the response tensors, including the effects of the Berry curvature (BC) and the orbital magnetic moment (OMM), which arise due to a nontrivial topology of the bandstructures. We elucidate the interplay of the BC-only and the OMM-dependent parts in the longitudinal and transverse (or Hall) components of the electric, thermal, and thermoelectric response tensors. Especially, for the co-planar transverse components of the response tensors, the OMM part acts exclusively in opposition (sync) with the BC-only part for the Weyl (multi-Weyl) semimetals.

Introduction

There has been an incredible amount of research work focussing on the investigations of the transport properties of semimetals, which are systems harbouring band-crossing points in the Brillouin zone (BZ). Two or more bands cross at the nodal points where the densities of states go to zero. Among the three-dimensional (3d) semimetals with twofold nodal points, the well-known examples include the Weyl semimetals (WSMs)1,2 and the multi-Weyl semimetals (mWSMs)3–5, whose bandstructures exhibit nontrivial topology quantified by the Berry phase. The nodal points for both the WSMs and the mWSMs are protected by the point-group symmetries of the crystal lattice4. In the language of the Berry curvature (BC) flux, each nodal point acts as a source or sink in the momentum space and, thus, acts as an analogue of the elusive magnetic monopole. The value of the monopole charge is equal to the Chern number arising from the Berry connection. A consequence of the Nielson-Ninomiya theorem6, applicable for systems in an odd number of spatial dimensions, the nodal points exist in pairs, with each pair carrying Chern numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm J$$\end{document}±J. Thus, the pair acts as a source and a sink of the BC flux. Since the sign of the monopole charge is called the chirality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ of the assoiciated node, the two nodes in a pair are of opposite chiralities (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = \pm 1$$\end{document}χ=±1). The values of J for Weyl (e.g., TaAs7–9 and HgTe-class materials10), double-Weyl (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {HgCr_2Se_4}$$\end{document}HgCr2Se411 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {SrSi_2}$$\end{document}SrSi212,13), and triple-Weyl nodes (e.g., transition-metal monochalcogenides14) are equal to one, two, and three, respectively.

Suppose we consider an experimental set-up with a WSM/mWSM semimetal subjected to an external uniform electric field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E along the x-axis and a uniform external magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B along the y-axis. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B is perpendicular to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E, a potential difference (known as the Hall voltage) will be generated along the z-axis. This phenomenon is the well-known Hall effect. However, if we apply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B making an angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \ne \pi /2 \text{ or } 3\pi/2$$\end{document}θ≠π/2or3π/2, the conventional Hall voltage induced from the Lorentz force is zero along the y-axis. Nonetheless, due to nontrivial Chern numbers, a voltage difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{PH}$$\end{document}VPH appears along this direction, as shown in Fig. 1. This is known as the planar Hall effect (PHE), arising due to the chiral anomaly15–21. The chiral anomaly refers to the phenomenon of charge pumping from one node to its conjugate when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}} \cdot {\textbf{B}} \ne 0$$\end{document}E·B≠0, originating from a local non-conservation of electric charge in the vicinity of an individual node, with the rate of change of the number density of chiral quasiparticles being proportional to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J\left( {\textbf{E}} \cdot {\textbf{B}} \right)$$\end{document}JE·B22,23. The associated transport coefficients, related to this set-up, are referred to as the longitudinal magnetoconductivity (LMC) and the planar Hall conductivity (PHC), which depend on the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ. In an analogous set-up, we observe the planar thermal Hall effect (PTHE), where we replace the external electric field by (or add) an external temperature gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\nabla }}_{{\textbf{r}}} T$$\end{document}∇rT. In this scenario, too, a potential difference is induced along the y-axis due to the chiral anomaly21,24 [cf. Fig. 1], with the response coefficients known as the longitudinal thermoelectric coefficient (LTEC) and transverse thermoelectric coefficient (TTEC). There has been a tremendous amount of efforts to determine the behaviour of these response tensors25–40.Figure 1Schematics showing the planar Hall (or planar thermal Hall) experimental set-up, where the sample is subjected to an external electric field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\, {\hat{\textbf{x}}}$$\end{document}Ex^ (and/or a temperature gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _x T\, {\hat{\textbf{x}}}$$\end{document}∂xTx^). An external magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B is applied such that it makes an angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ with the existing electric field (and/or the temperature gradient). In addition, the sample is considered to be under the influence of a mechanical strain, whose effect is incorporated via an artificial chiral gauge field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5, making an angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _5$$\end{document}θ5 with the x-axis. The resulting planar Hall (or planar thermal Hall) voltage, generated along the y-axis, is indicated by the symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{PH}$$\end{document}VPH.

Schematics showing the planar Hall (or planar thermal Hall) experimental set-up, where the sample is subjected to an external electric field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\, {\hat{\textbf{x}}}$$\end{document}Ex^ (and/or a temperature gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _x T\, {\hat{\textbf{x}}}$$\end{document}∂xTx^). An external magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B is applied such that it makes an angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ with the existing electric field (and/or the temperature gradient). In addition, the sample is considered to be under the influence of a mechanical strain, whose effect is incorporated via an artificial chiral gauge field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5, making an angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _5$$\end{document}θ5 with the x-axis. The resulting planar Hall (or planar thermal Hall) voltage, generated along the y-axis, is indicated by the symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{PH}$$\end{document}VPH.

In a planar Hall (or thermal Hall) set-up, if a semimetal is subjected to mechanical strain, it induces elastic deformations of the material. The elastic deformations couple to the electronic degrees of freedom (i.e., quasiparticles) in such a way that they can be modelled as pseudogauge fields in the semimetals37,41–47. The form of these elastic gauge fields shows that they couple to the quasiparticles of the Weyl fermions with opposite chiralities with opposite signs37,44–46,48,49. Due to the chiral nature of the coupling between the emergent vector fields and the itinerant fermionic carriers, this provides an example of axial gauge fields in three dimensions. This is to be contrasted with the actual electromagnetic fields, which couple to all the nodes with the same sign. While a uniform pseudomagnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 can be generated when a WSM/mWSM nanowire is put under torsion, a pseudoelectric field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}_5$$\end{document}E5 appears on dynamically stretching and compressing the crystal along an axis (which can be achieved, for example, by driving longitudinal sound waves)46. Direct evidence of the generation of such pseudoelectromagnetic fields in doped semimetals has been obtained in experiments50. In an earlier work by the two of us38, the response tensors have been computed for WSMs and mWSMs, but neglecting the orbital magnetic moment (OMM)51,52, which is another artifact of a nontrivial topology in the bandstructures. Although the effects of OMM were included in the computations of Ref.37, only the electric conductivity at zero temperature was studied. Hence, in this paper, we derive all the relevant electric, thermal, and thermoelectric response tensors, associated with the planar Hall and planar thermal set-ups, which constitute a complete description incorporating both the BC and the OMM.

As shown in Fig. 1, the co-planar \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi = {\textbf{B}} + \chi \,{\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}Bχ=B+χB5 set-ups considered here consist of a nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 and a nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B in the xy-plane. These two parts of the effective magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ are oriented at the angles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _5$$\end{document}θ5, respectively, with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _{{\textbf{r}}} T$$\end{document}∇rT) applied along the x-axis. In other words, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}} = B \left( \cos \theta , \sin \theta , 0 \right)$$\end{document}B=Bcosθ,sinθ,0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5 = B_5 \left( \cos \theta _5, \sin \theta _5, 0 \right)$$\end{document}B5=B5cosθ5,sinθ5,0, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \equiv |{\textbf{B}}|$$\end{document}B≡|B| and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_5 \equiv |{\textbf{B}}_5|$$\end{document}B5≡|B5|. We consider the weak magnetic field limit with low values of B and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_5$$\end{document}B5, such that the formation of the Landau levels can be ignored, and the magnetoelectric, magnetothermal, and magnetothermoelectric response can be derived using the semiclassical Boltzmann formalism. The paper is organized as follows: In Sect. "Model", we describe the low-energy effective Hamiltonians for the WSMs and mWSMs. In Sects. "Magnetoelectric conductivity", "Magnetothermoelectric conductivity", and "Magnetothermal coefficient", we show the explicit expressions of the in-plane components of the response tensors, and discuss their behaviour in some relevant parameter regimes. Finally, we conclude with a summary and outlook in Sect. "Summary and future perspectives". The appendices are devoted to explaining the details of the intermediate steps used to derive the final expressions in the main text.

Model

The band velocity of the chiral quasiparticles is given by3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\varvec{v}}}^{(0)}_{\chi , s}( {\textbf{k}}) \equiv \nabla _{{\textbf{k}}} \varepsilon _{\chi , s} ({\textbf{k}}) = - In our plots, we have used \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_\perp = v_z$$\end{document}v⊥=vz (from the table entry), leading to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{2}=3.9 \times 10^{-5}$$\end{document}α2=3.9×10-5 eV\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document}-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{3}=2.298 \times 10^{-6}$$\end{document}α3=2.298×10-6 eV\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-2}$$\end{document}-2. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=2$$\end{document}J=2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=3$$\end{document}J=3, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_\perp$$\end{document}v⊥ has been set equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_z$$\end{document}vz for the sake of simplicity, while the isotropic dispersion for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J =1$$\end{document}J=1 has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{\perp } = v_z$$\end{document}v⊥=vz automatically.

Response tensors using the Boltzmann formalism

Using the semiclassical Boltzmann formalism57,58, the transport coefficients can be determined in the weak \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\textbf{B}}_\chi |$$\end{document}|Bχ| limit, which applies to the regime of small cyclotron frequency, implying that the Landau-level quantization can be ignored. The detailed steps can be found in the Appendix A of Ref.38, which we do not repeat here for the sake of brevity. Moreover, we work with the relation-time approximation for the collision integral, taking a simplistic momentum-independent relaxation time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}τ. Furthermore, we assume that intranode scatterings are dominant over the internode scattering processes, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}τ corresponds only to the former.

Let the contributions to the average electric and thermal current densities from the quasiparticles, associated with the node of chirality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ, be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{J}}}^\chi$$\end{document}Jχ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{J}}}^{\text{th},\chi }$$\end{document}Jth,χ, respectively. The response matrix, which relates the resulting generalized currents to the driving electric potential gradient and/or temperature gradient, can be expressed as7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{pmatrix} J_i^\chi \\ {J}_i^{\text{th},\chi } \end{pmatrix}&= \sum \limits _j \begin{pmatrix} \sigma _{ij}^\chi &{} \alpha ^\chi _{ i j } \\ T\, \alpha ^\chi _{ij} &{} \ell ^\chi _{ i j } \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} E_j \\ -{ \partial _{j} T } \end{pmatrix} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}JiχJith,χ=∑jσijχαijχTαijχℓijχEj-∂jT.Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^\chi _{ij}$$\end{document}σijχ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^\chi _{ij}$$\end{document}αijχ represent the components of the magnetoelectric conductivity tensor (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^\chi$$\end{document}σχ) and the magnetothermoelectric conductivity tensor (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^\chi$$\end{document}αχ), respectively. While \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^\chi$$\end{document}αχ determines the Peltier (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^\chi$$\end{document}Πχ), Seebeck (S), and Nernst coefficients, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^\chi$$\end{document}ℓχ is the linear response tensor relating the heat current density to the temperature gradient, at a vanishing electric field. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^\chi$$\end{document}Sχ , \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^\chi$$\end{document}Πχ, and the magnetothermal conductivity tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^\chi$$\end{document}κχ (which provides the coefficients between the heat current density and the temperature gradient at vanishing electric current) can be extracted from the coefficients on the right-hand-side of Eq. The expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^{\chi }_{ij}$$\end{document}σijχ, shown above, includes the effects of the BC and the OMM. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{0}$$\end{document}f0 is the equilibrium Fermi-Dirac distribution at temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T =1/\beta$$\end{document}T=1/β and chemical potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu_\chi$$\end{document}μχ. As discussed in Refs.37,38, while a purely physical magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B gives a quadratic-dependence of the response on the overall magnetic field, inclusion of a nonzero axial part \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 opens up the possibility of generating linear and parabolic behaviour of the response tensors.

Analogous to Eq. (14), we have the general expressions2418\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\alpha } ^{\chi }_{ij}&= e \, \tau \int \frac{ d^3 {\textbf{k}} }{ (2\, \pi )^3 } \, D_{\chi } \, \left[ v_{\chi i } + e \, ( {{\varvec{v}}}_{\chi } \cdot {\varvec{\Omega }}_{\chi } ) \, B_{\chi i} \right] \, \left[ v_{\chi j } + e \, ( {{\varvec{v}}}_{\chi } \cdot {\varvec{\Omega }}_{\chi } ) \, B_{\chi j} \right] \, \frac{{\mathcal {E}}_{\chi } - \mu }{T} \, \frac{\partial f_{0} ({\mathcal {E}}_{\chi }) }{ \partial {\mathcal {E}}_{\chi } } , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αijχ=eτ∫d3k(2π)3Dχvχi+e(vχ·Ωχ)Bχivχj+e(vχ·Ωχ)BχjEχ-μT∂f0(Eχ)∂Eχ,and19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ell ^\chi _{ij}&= - \,\tau \int \frac{ d^3 {\textbf{k}} }{ (2\, \pi )^3 } \, D_{\chi } \, \left[ v_{\chi i } + e \, ( {{\varvec{v}}}_{\chi } \cdot {\varvec{\Omega }}_{\chi } ) \, B_{\chi i} \right] \, \left[ v_{\chi j } + e \, ( {{\varvec{v}}}_{\chi } \cdot {\varvec{\Omega }}_{\chi } ) \, B_{\chi j} \right] \, \frac{ ( {\mathcal {E}}_{\chi } - \mu )^2 }{T} \, \frac{\partial f_{0} ({\mathcal {E}}_{\chi }) }{ \partial {\mathcal {E}}_{\chi } } , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ℓijχ=-τ∫d3k(2π)3Dχvχi+e(vχ·Ωχ)Bχivχj+e(vχ·Ωχ)Bχj(Eχ-μ)2T∂f0(Eχ)∂Eχ,respectively. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^\chi$$\end{document}ℓχ determines the first term in the magnetothermal conductivity tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^\chi$$\end{document}κχ, we will often loosely refer to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^\chi$$\end{document}ℓχ itself as the magnetothermal coefficient.

Magnetoelectric conductivity

Working in the weak-in-magnetic-field limit, which allows expansions in the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_{\chi }$$\end{document}Bχ, the terms in Eq. (14) are disentangled into a sum of three terms, having distinct origins, as follows:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma ^{\chi }_{ij}&= \sigma ^{0, \chi }_{ij} + \sigma ^{\Omega , \chi }_{ij} + \sigma ^{m, \chi }_{ij} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σijχ=σij0,χ+σijΩ,χ+σijm,χ.Here,21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma ^{0, \chi }_{ij} = - The symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{ {\textbf{e}} }_{i}$$\end{document}e^i represents the unit vector along the Cartesian coordinate axis labelled by i.

The longitudinal and transverse components of the magnetoelectric conductivity tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^\chi$$\end{document}σχ (i.e., the LMC and the PHC) are computed from the starting expression shown in Eq. (14). The details of the intermediate steps are given in Appendices B and C. Before delving into the investigation of the behaviour of these response coefficients in the subsequent subsections, first let us analyze the relation between the current and the axial electromagnetic fields.

For the quasiparticles with chirality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ, we have the electric current contribution25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_i^{\chi } (\mu ) =\sigma _{ij}^{\chi } (\mu )\,E_{\chi j}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jiχ(μ)=σijχ(μ)Eχj,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{ij}^{\chi }$$\end{document}σijχ is given by Eq. (14). For two conjugate nodes with chemical potential values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _+$$\end{document}μ+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _-$$\end{document}μ- (as shown schematically in Fig. 4 for WSMs), we define the total and axial currents as26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} &{\varvec{J}} (\mu _+, \mu _-) = \sum _{\chi =\pm 1}{\varvec{J}}^{\chi }(\mu _\chi ) ={\varvec{J}}^{+} (\mu _+) + {\varvec{J}}^{-} (\mu _-) \nonumber \\ &\text {and } {\varvec{J}}_5 (\mu _+, \mu _-) = \sum _{\chi =\pm 1}\chi \, {\varvec{J}}^{\chi } (\mu _\chi ) ={\varvec{J}}^{+} (\mu _+) -{\varvec{J}}^{-}(\mu _+) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J(μ+,μ-)=∑χ=±1Jχ(μχ)=J+(μ+)+J-(μ-)andJ5(μ+,μ-)=∑χ=±1χJχ(μχ)=J+(μ+)-J-(μ+),respectively. These suggest to introduce analogous expressions for the conductivity tensors as27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma _{ij} (\mu _+, \mu _-) = \sum _{\chi =\pm 1}\sigma _{ij}^{ \chi } (\mu _\chi ) \text { and } \sigma _{5ij}=\sum _{\chi =\pm 1}\chi \, \sigma _{ij}^{ \chi }(\mu _\chi ) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σij(μ+,μ-)=∑χ=±1σijχ(μχ)andσ5ij=∑χ=±1χσijχ(μχ).Figure 4A pair of conjugate Weyl nodes with the corresponding chemical potentials tuned to two different values, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _+$$\end{document}μ+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _-$$\end{document}μ-.

A pair of conjugate Weyl nodes with the corresponding chemical potentials tuned to two different values, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _+$$\end{document}μ+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _-$$\end{document}μ-.

Using Eq. (27), we find that28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_i (\mu _+, \mu _-)&=\sigma _{ij}^{+} (\mu _+)\,E_{+j} +\sigma _{ij}^{-} (\mu _-) \, E_{-j} = \sigma _{ij} (\mu _+, \mu _-)\,E_j + \sigma _{5ij} (\mu _+, \mu _-)\, E_{5j} \nonumber \\ \text {and } J_{5i} (\mu _+, \mu _-)&= \sigma _{ij}^{+} (\mu _+)\, E_{+j}-\sigma _{ij}^{-}(\mu _-) \,E_{-j} =\sigma _{5ij} (\mu _+, \mu _-) \,E_j + \sigma _{ij} (\mu _+, \mu _-) \,E_{5j}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ji(μ+,μ-)=σij+(μ+)E+j+σij-(μ-)E-j=σij(μ+,μ-)Ej+σ5ij(μ+,μ-)E5jandJ5i(μ+,μ-)=σij+(μ+)E+j-σij-(μ-)E-j=σ5ij(μ+,μ-)Ej+σij(μ+,μ-)E5j,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}_5$$\end{document}E5 is an axial pseudoelectric field, which can be generated artificially (as explained in the introduction). For the node with chirality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ, analogous to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ, the physical \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E and the axial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}} _5$$\end{document}E5 add up to give the effective electric field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}} _{\chi }= {\textbf{E}} +\chi \,{\textbf{E}}_5$$\end{document}Eχ=E+χE5, reflecting the dependence on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ. In this paper, we deal with the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}_5 ={\textbf{0}}$$\end{document}E5=0.

From the above expressions for the total and axial currents, we now discuss the behaviour of the total and axial LMC and PHC as functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ, which is the angle between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B, with the former chosen to be directed along the x-axis [cf. Fig. 1]. For the illustration of the behaviour of the response, we define29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Sigma _{ij} ({\textbf{B}}_\chi ) = \sigma _{ij} (\mu _+, \mu _- ) - \sigma _{ij} ( \mu _+, \mu _- ) \Big \vert _{{\textbf{B}}_\chi = {\varvec{0}} }\nonumber \\ & \text {and } \Sigma _{5ij} ({\textbf{B}}_\chi ) = \sigma _{5ij} (\mu _+, \mu _-) -\sigma _{5ij} (\mu _+, \mu _-) \Big \vert _{{\textbf{B}}_\chi = {\varvec{0}} } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Σij(Bχ)=σij(μ+,μ-)-σij(μ+,μ-)|Bχ=0andΣ5ij(Bχ)=σ5ij(μ+,μ-)-σ5ij(μ+,μ-)|Bχ=0for the total and axial conductivity tensor components, respectively, after subtracting off the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ-independent parts. We denote the parts connected with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^{\Omega , \chi }_{ij}$$\end{document}σijΩ,χ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^{m, \chi }_{ij}$$\end{document}σijm,χ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \Sigma _{ij}^\Omega , \, \Sigma _{5ij}^\Omega )$$\end{document}(ΣijΩ,Σ5ijΩ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \Sigma _{ij}^m, \, \Sigma _{5ij}^m )$$\end{document}(Σijm,Σ5ijm), respectively. Therefore, if the OMM is not considered, each of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$( \Sigma _{ij}^m, \, \Sigma _{5ij}^m )$$\end{document}(Σijm,Σ5ijm) goes to zero.

Longitudinal magnetoconductivity

Using the explicit expressions derived in Appendix B, we have30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma ^{\chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )&= \sigma ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) + \sigma ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) + \sigma ^{m, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σxxχ(μχ)=σxx0,χ(μχ)+σxxΩ,χ(μχ)+σxxm,χ(μχ),where31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} &\sigma ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )= \frac{ e^2 \, \tau \, J }{ 6 \, \pi ^2 \, v_z } \, \Lambda _{2} (\mu _\chi ) \,,\nonumber \\ & \sigma ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) = \frac{e^4 \, \tau \, v_{z } \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, \Lambda _{ - From Fig. 5, we find that (1) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_x^{bc} (J)$$\end{document}gxbc(J) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_y^{bc} (J)$$\end{document}gybc(J) are positive for all J-values; (2) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_x^m (J)$$\end{document}gxm(J) is negative for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=1$$\end{document}J=1 and positive for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=2, \,3$$\end{document}J=2,3; (3) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_y^m (J)$$\end{document}gym(J) is negative for all J-values. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=1$$\end{document}J=1, the OMM acts in opposition to the BC-only term for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\chi x}$$\end{document}Bχx-part, and reduces the overall response. On the other hand, for the mWSMs, the OMM adds up to the BC-only term for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\chi x}$$\end{document}Bχx-part, thus increasing the overall response. For the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\chi y}$$\end{document}Bχy-part, the OMM and the BC-only parts always have opposite signs and, thus, tend to reduce the overall response’s magnitude. Therefore, for a WSM, the OMM part always reduces the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^{\chi }_{xx}$$\end{document}σxxχ by adding a negative contribution.Figure 5Comparison of the values of the functions defined in Eqs. In order to estimate the effects of the OMM (which was neglected in many earlier works), we plot the behaviour of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{xx}^\Omega$$\end{document}ΣxxΩ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{xx}^m$$\end{document}Σxxm, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{5xx}^\Omega$$\end{document}Σ5xxΩ, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{5xx}^m$$\end{document}Σ5xxm in Figs. 6 and 7. The interplay of the BC-only and the OMM-induced parts are illustrated via some representative parameter values. In agreement with our comparison of the g-values, we find that for a WSM, the OMM can even change the sign of the response, depending on the net magnetic field. However, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J= 2, \, 3$$\end{document}J=2,3, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_x^m$$\end{document}gxm and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_y^m$$\end{document}gym-parts have opposite signs — hence, their combined effects may increase or reduce the overall response. From Fig. 6, we find that, turning on a nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5-part changes the periodicity, with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ, from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi$$\end{document}π to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \pi.$$\end{document}2π. This is completely expected because the axial pseudomagnetic field causes linear-in-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \cos \theta$$\end{document}Bcosθ and/or linear-in-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\sin \theta$$\end{document}Bsinθ terms to appear, in addition to the quadratic-in-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_i$$\end{document}Bi dependence of the untilted WSMs/mWSMs. Figure 6The total and axial combinations of the LMC (in units of eV) for the two conjugate nodes, defined in Eq. Planar Hall conductivity

Using the explicit expressions derived in Appendix C, we have33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma ^{\chi }_{yx} (\mu _\chi ) = \sigma ^{\chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) = \sigma ^{0, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) + \sigma ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) + \sigma ^{m, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi )\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σyxχ(μχ)=σxyχ(μχ)=σxy0,χ(μχ)+σxyΩ,χ(μχ)+σxym,χ(μχ),where34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma ^{0, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi )&= 0 \,, \quad \sigma ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) = \frac{e^4 \, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, \Lambda _{ - From Fig. 5, we find that (1) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{bc} (J)$$\end{document}fbc(J) is positive for all J-values; (2) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^m (J)$$\end{document}fm(J) is negative for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=1$$\end{document}J=1 and positive for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=2, \,3$$\end{document}J=2,3. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=1$$\end{document}J=1, the OMM thus acts in opposition to the BC-only term, and reduces the magnitude of the overall PHC. In contrast, for the mWSMs, the OMM adds up to the BC-only term, thus increasing the overall response.

In order to estimate the effects of the OMM, we plot the behaviour of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{xy}^\Omega$$\end{document}ΣxyΩ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{xy}^m$$\end{document}Σxym, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{5xy}^\Omega$$\end{document}Σ5xyΩ, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{5xy}^m$$\end{document}Σ5xym in Figs. 8 and 9. The interplay of the BC-only and the OMM-induced parts are illustrated via the same parameter values as considered for the LMC. In agreement with our comparison of the f-values, we find that for a WSM, the OMM always reduces the response. On the other hand, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J= 2, \, 3$$\end{document}J=2,3, the effect of OMM is to enhance the overall response. Analogous to the LMC, Fig. 8 shows that a nonzero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5-part changes the periodicity with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi$$\end{document}π to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \pi$$\end{document}2π, which results from the emergence of terms linearly proportional to the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B, rather than just the quadratic ones. Using the above expressions for the function defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{ij}^\chi = F_{ij}^\chi \, \frac{{\mathcal {E}}_{\chi } - \mu }{T}$$\end{document}Gijχ=FijχEχ-μT, the weak-field expansion gives us39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{ij}^\chi&= F_{ij}^\chi \,\frac{\varepsilon _\chi - \mu }{T} + \left[ F_{ij}^{0, \chi } + F_{ij}^{1,\Omega , \chi } + F_{ij}^{1,m, \chi } \right] \frac{ \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } }{T} + \mathcal {O}( |{ {\textbf{B}}}_{\chi }|^3 ) \nonumber \\&= G_{ij}^{0,\chi } + G_{ij} ^{1,\Omega ,\chi } + G_{ij} ^{1, m,\chi } + G_{ij} ^{2,\Omega ,\chi } + G_{ij} ^{2,m,\chi } + G_{ij} ^{2,(\Omega , m),\chi } + \mathcal {O}( |{ {\textbf{B}}}_{\chi }|^3 ) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gijχ=Fijχεχ-μT+Fij0,χ+Fij1,Ω,χ+Fij1,m,χεχ(m)T+O(|Bχ|3)=Gij0,χ+Gij1,Ω,χ+Gij1,m,χ+Gij2,Ω,χ+Gij2,m,χ+Gij2,(Ω,m),χ+O(|Bχ|3).

Here,40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{ij}^{0,\chi }&= \frac{ F_{ij}^{0, \chi } \left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) }{T} \,,\quad G_{ij} ^{1,\Omega ,\chi } = \frac{ F_{ij} ^{1,\Omega , \chi } \left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) }{T} \,,\quad \nonumber \\ G_{ij} ^{1,m,\chi }&= \frac{ F_{ij}^{0, \chi }\, \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } }{T} + \frac{ F_{ij} ^{1,m, \chi } \left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) }{T} \,, \quad G_{ij} ^{2,\Omega ,\chi } = \frac{ F_{ij} ^{2,\Omega , \chi } \left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) }{T} \,,\nonumber \\ G_{ij} ^{2,m,\chi }&= \frac{ F_{ij}^{1, m, \chi }\, \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } }{T} + \frac{ F_{ij} ^{ 2,m, \chi } \left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) }{T} \,, \quad G_{ij} ^{2,(\Omega , m),\chi } = \frac{ F_{ij}^{1, \Omega , \chi }\, \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } }{T} + \frac{ F_{ij} ^{ 2,(\Omega , m), \chi } \left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) }{T}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gij0,χ=Fij0,χεχ-μT,Gij1,Ω,χ=Fij1,Ω,χεχ-μT,Gij1,m,χ=Fij0,χεχ(m)T+Fij1,m,χεχ-μT,Gij2,Ω,χ=Fij2,Ω,χεχ-μT,Gij2,m,χ=Fij1,m,χεχ(m)T+Fij2,m,χεχ-μT,Gij2,(Ω,m),χ=Fij1,Ω,χεχ(m)T+Fij2,(Ω,m),χεχ-μT,with the parts named in a similar spirit as done for the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{ij}^\chi$$\end{document}Fijχ.

Defining a third function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{ij}^\chi = G_{ij} \, f'_{0} ( {\mathcal {E}}_{\chi } )$$\end{document}Hijχ=Gijf0′(Eχ), and using Eq. (13), its expansion turns out to be41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{ij}^\chi&= G_{ij}^\chi \, f'_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi ) + \left[ G_{ij}^{0, \chi } + G_{ij}^{1,\Omega , \chi } + G_{ij}^{1,m, \chi } \right] \, \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } \, f^{\prime \prime }_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi ) + \frac{ G_{ij}^{0, \chi } \,\left( \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } \right) ^2 \, f^{\prime \prime \prime }_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi )}{2} + \mathcal {O}( |{ {\textbf{B}}}_{\chi }|^3) \nonumber \\&= G_{ij}^{0,\chi } + G_{ij}^{1,\Omega ,\chi } + G_{ij}^{1, m,\chi } + G_{ij}^{2,\Omega ,\chi } + G_{ij}^{2,m,\chi } + G_{ij}^{2,(\Omega , m),\chi } + \mathcal {O}( |{ {\textbf{B}}}_{\chi }|^3) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hijχ=Gijχf0′(εχ)+Gij0,χ+Gij1,Ω,χ+Gij1,m,χεχ(m)f0″(εχ)+Gij0,χεχ(m)2f0″′(εχ)2+O(|Bχ|3)=Gij0,χ+Gij1,Ω,χ+Gij1,m,χ+Gij2,Ω,χ+Gij2,m,χ+Gij2,(Ω,m),χ+O(|Bχ|3).

Here,42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{ij}^{0,\chi }&= G_{ij}^{0, \chi } \, f'_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi )\,, \quad H_{ij}^{1,\Omega ,\chi } = G_{ij}^{1,\Omega ,\chi } \, f'_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi )\,,\quad H_{ij}^{1,m,\chi } = G_{ij}^{0, \chi } \, \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } \, f^{\prime \prime }_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi ) + G_{ij}^{1,m,\chi } \, f'_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi )\,,\nonumber \\ H_{ij}^{2 ,\Omega ,\chi }&= G_{ij}^{2 ,\Omega ,\chi } \, f'_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi )\,, \quad H_{ij}^{2 ,m ,\chi } = \frac{G_{ij}^{0, \chi } \left( \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } \right) ^2 f^{\prime \prime \prime }_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi ) }{2} + G_{ij}^{ 1, m, \chi } \, \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } \, f^{\prime \prime }_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi ) + G_{ij}^{2 , m ,\chi } \, f'_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi )\,, \nonumber \\ H_{ij}^{2,(\Omega , m),\chi }&= G_{ij}^{1,\Omega ,\chi } \, \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } \, f^{\prime \prime }_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi ) + G_{ij}^{2,(\Omega , m),\chi } \, f'_{0} ( \varepsilon _\chi )\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hij0,χ=Gij0,χf0′(εχ),Hij1,Ω,χ=Gij1,Ω,χf0′(εχ),Hij1,m,χ=Gij0,χεχ(m)f0″(εχ)+Gij1,m,χf0′(εχ),Hij2,Ω,χ=Gij2,Ω,χf0′(εχ),Hij2,m,χ=Gij0,χεχ(m)2f0″′(εχ)2+Gij1,m,χεχ(m)f0″(εχ)+Gij2,m,χf0′(εχ),Hij2,(Ω,m),χ=Gij1,Ω,χεχ(m)f0″(εχ)+Gij2,(Ω,m),χf0′(εχ).

The nomenclature of these parts follow the same scheme as that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{ij}^\chi$$\end{document}Fijχ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{ij}^\chi$$\end{document}Gijχ.
