Longitudinal magnetothermoelectric coefficient

Using the explicit expressions derived in Appendix D, we have46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha ^{\chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) = \alpha ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) + \alpha ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) + \alpha ^{m, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αxxχ(μχ)=αxx0,χ(μχ)+αxxΩ,χ(μχ)+αxxm,χ(μχ),where47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )&= - \frac{ e \, \tau \, J \, \mu _\chi }{ 9 \, v_z \, \beta } \,, \quad \alpha ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) = \frac{ \sqrt{\pi } \, e^3 \, \tau \, v_{z } \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, \Gamma \big ( 2-\frac{1}{J}\big ) }{ 192 \, \beta \, \mu _\chi ^{1 + \frac{2}{ J} } \, \Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big )} \, \frac{ g_x^{bc} \, B_{\chi x}^2 + g_y^{bc} \, B_{\chi y}^2}{J} \, ,\nonumber \\ \alpha ^{m, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )&= \frac{ \sqrt{\pi } \, e^3 \, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, \Gamma \big ( 2 - \frac{1}{J} \big ) }{ 192 \, \mu _\chi ^{1+\frac{2}{J}} \, \beta \, \Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big ) } \, \frac{ g_x^m \, B_{\chi x}^2 + g_y^m \, B_{\chi y}^2}{J} \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αxx0,χ(μχ)=-eτJμχ9vzβ,αxxΩ,χ(μχ)=πe3τvzαJ2JΓ(2-1J)192βμχ1+2JΓ(92-1J)gxbcBχx2+gybcBχy2J,αxxm,χ(μχ)=πe3τvzαJ2JΓ(2-1J)192μχ1+2JβΓ(92-1J)gxmBχx2+gymBχy2J.

Transverse magnetothermoelectric coefficient

Using the explicit expressions derived in Appendix E, we have48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha ^{\chi }_{yx} (\mu _\chi ) = \alpha ^{\chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) = \alpha ^{0, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) + \alpha ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) + \alpha ^{m, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi )\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αyxχ(μχ)=αxyχ(μχ)=αxy0,χ(μχ)+αxyΩ,χ(μχ)+αxym,χ(μχ),where49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\alpha ^{0, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) = 0 \,, \quad \alpha ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) = \frac{ \sqrt{\pi } \, e^3 \, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, \Gamma \big ( 2-\frac{1}{J} \big ) }{ 96 \, \beta \, \mu _\chi ^{ 1+\frac{2}{J}} \,\Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big ) } \, \frac{ f^{bc} (J) }{J} \, B_{\chi x} \, B_{\chi y} \, ,\nonumber \\&\alpha ^{m, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) = \frac{ \sqrt{\pi } \, e^3 \, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, \Gamma \big ( 2-\frac{1}{J} \big )}{96 \, \beta \, \mu _\chi ^{ 1+\frac{2}{J}} \, \Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big )} \, \frac{ f^m (J) }{J} \, B_{\chi x} \, B_{\chi y} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αxy0,χ(μχ)=0,αxyΩ,χ(μχ)=πe3τvzαJ2JΓ(2-1J)96βμχ1+2JΓ(92-1J)fbc(J)JBχxBχy,αxym,χ(μχ)=πe3τvzαJ2JΓ(2-1J)96βμχ1+2JΓ(92-1J)fm(J)JBχxBχy.

Mott relation

From the explicit expressions of the in-plane longitudinal and transverse components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^\chi$$\end{document}σχ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^\chi$$\end{document}αχ, which we have derived [cf. Eqs. (31), (34), (47), and (49)], we can immediately infer that the relation50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{\mu _\chi } \sigma _{ij}^{\chi } (\mu _\chi ) = - \frac{3\, e\, \beta }{\pi ^2} \, \alpha _{ij}^{\chi } (\mu _\chi ) + \mathcal {O}(\beta ^{-2}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂μχσijχ(μχ)=-3eβπ2αijχ(μχ)+O(β-2)is satisfied. This is equivalent to satisfying the Mott relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{ij}^\chi (\mu _\chi ) = - \frac{ \pi ^2 }{3 \, e \, \beta } \,\partial _{\mu _\chi } \sigma _{ij}^\chi (\mu _\chi )$$\end{document}αijχ(μχ)=-π23eβ∂μχσijχ(μχ), which holds in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}β→∞57, and can be derived mathematically through the Sommerfeld expansion. Hence, we find that the Mott relation continues to hold even in the presence of OMM, agreeing with the results in Ref.59, where generic settings have been considered.

Due to the Mott relation, the nature of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{ij}^\Omega$$\end{document}AijΩ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{ij}^m$$\end{document}Aijm can be readily inferred from that of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{ij}^\Omega$$\end{document}ΣijΩ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{ij}^m$$\end{document}Σijm, which we have already discussed in the preceding section. Nevertheless, we provide here some representative plots of the (1) longitudinal planar components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{xx}^\Omega$$\end{document}AxxΩ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{xx}^m$$\end{document}Axxm, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{5xx}^\Omega$$\end{document}A5xxΩ, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{5xx}^m$$\end{document}A5xxm in Figs. 10 and 11; Magnetothermal coefficient

Using the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{ij}$$\end{document}Fij, whose Taylor-expanded form has been shown in Eq. (37), we now define the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\tilde{G}}}_{ij}^\chi = F_{ij}^\chi \left( {\mathcal {E}}_{\chi } - \mu \right) ^2 / \,T = F_{ij}^\chi \left[ \varepsilon _\chi + \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m) } - \mu \right] ^2 /\,T$$\end{document}G~ijχ=FijχEχ-μ2/T=Fijχεχ+εχ(m)-μ2/T. Its weak-field expansion is given by51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\tilde{G}}}_{ij}^\chi&= F_{ij}^\chi \,\frac{\left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) ^2 }{T} + 2 \left[ F_{ij}^{0, \chi } + F_{ij}^{1,\Omega , \chi } + F_{ij}^{1,m, \chi } \right] \, \frac{ \left( \varepsilon _\chi - \mu \right) \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m)} }{T} + F_{ij}^{0, \chi } \,\frac{ \left( \varepsilon _\chi ^{ (m)} \right) ^2 }{T} + \mathcal {O}( |{ {\textbf{B}}}_{\chi }|^3 ) \nonumber \\&= {{\tilde{G}}}_{ij}^{0,\chi } + {{\tilde{G}}}_{ij} ^{1,\Omega ,\chi } + G_{ij} ^{1, m,\chi } + {{\tilde{G}}}_{ij} ^{2,\Omega ,\chi } + {{\tilde{G}}}_{ij} ^{2,m,\chi } + {{\tilde{G}}}_{ij} ^{2,(\Omega , m),\chi } + \mathcal {O}( |{ {\textbf{B}}}_{\chi }|^3) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G~ijχ=Fijχεχ-μ2T+2Fij0,χ+Fij1,Ω,χ+Fij1,m,χεχ-μεχ(m)T+Fij0,χεχ(m)2T+O(|Bχ|3)=G~ij0,χ+G~ij1,Ω,χ+Gij1,m,χ+G~ij2,Ω,χ+G~ij2,m,χ+G~ij2,(Ω,m),χ+O(|Bχ|3).

\end{aligned}$$\end{document}H~ij0,χ=G~ij0,χf0′(εχ),H~ij1,Ω,χ=G~ij1,Ω,χf0′(εχ),H~ij1,m,χ=G~ij0,χεχ(m)f0″(εχ)+G~ij1,m,χf0′(εχ),H~ij2,Ω,χ=G~ij2,Ω,χf0′(εχ),H~ij2,m,χ=G~ij0,χεχ(m)2f0″′(εχ)2+G~ij1,m,χεχ(m)f0″(εχ)+G~ij2,m,χf0′(εχ),H~ij2,(Ω,m),χ=G~ij1,Ω,χεχ(m)f0″(εχ)+G~ij2,(Ω,m),χf0′(εχ).

Longitudinal magnetothermal coefficient

Using the explicit expressions derived in Appendix F, we have60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ell ^{\chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) = \ell ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) + \ell ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) + \ell ^{m, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ℓxxχ(μχ)=ℓxx0,χ(μχ)+ℓxxΩ,χ(μχ)+ℓxxm,χ(μχ),where61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ell ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )&= \frac{J \, \tau \,\mu _\chi ^2\, T}{ 18 \, v_z } \,, \nonumber \\ \ell ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) &= \frac{ \sqrt{\pi }\, e^2\, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, T}{3 \times 128 \, \mu _\chi ^{\frac{2}{J}} } \,\frac{ \Gamma \big (2 - \frac{1}{J} \big ) }{ \Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big ) } \left[ g_x^{bc} (J) \, B_{\chi x}^2 + g_y^{bc} (J)\, B_{\chi y}^2 \right], \nonumber \\ \ell ^{ m, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )&= \frac{ \sqrt{\pi }\, e^2 \, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \,T }{ 3 \times 128 \, \mu _\chi ^{ \frac{2}{J} } } \,\frac{ \Gamma \big (2 - \frac{1}{J} \big ) }{ \Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big ) } \left[ g_x^m (J) \, B_{\chi x}^2 + g_y^{m} (J)\, B_{\chi y}^2 \right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ℓxx0,χ(μχ)=Jτμχ2T18vz,ℓxxΩ,χ(μχ)=πe2τvzαJ2JT3×128μχ2JΓ(2-1J)Γ(92-1J)gxbc(J)Bχx2+gybc(J)Bχy2,ℓxxm,χ(μχ)=πe2τvzαJ2JT3×128μχ2JΓ(2-1J)Γ(92-1J)gxm(J)Bχx2+gym(J)Bχy2.

Transverse magnetothermal coefficient

Using the explicit expressions derived in Appendix G, we have62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ell ^{\chi }_{yx} (\mu _\chi ) = \ell ^{\chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) = \ell ^{0, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) + \ell ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) + \ell ^{m, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi )\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ℓyxχ(μχ)=ℓxyχ(μχ)=ℓxy0,χ(μχ)+ℓxyΩ,χ(μχ)+ℓxym,χ(μχ),where63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ell ^{0, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi )&= 0 \,, \nonumber \\ \ell ^{\Omega , \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi ) &= \frac{ \sqrt{\pi }\, e^2\, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, T}{3 \times 64 \, \mu _\chi ^{\frac{2}{J}} } \,\frac{ \Gamma \big (2 - \frac{1}{J} \big ) }{ \Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big ) } \, f^{bc} (J) \,B_{\chi x} \, B_{\chi y} \,,\nonumber \\ \ell ^{m, \chi }_{xy} (\mu _\chi )&= \frac{ \sqrt{\pi }\, e^2\, \tau \, v_z \, \alpha _{J}^{ \frac{2}{J} } \, T}{3 \times 64 \, \mu _\chi ^{\frac{2}{J}} } \,\frac{ \Gamma \big (2 - \frac{1}{J} \big ) }{ \Gamma \big ( \frac{9}{2} - \frac{1}{J} \big ) } \, f^{m} (J) \,B_{\chi x} \, B_{\chi y} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ℓxy0,χ(μχ)=0,ℓxyΩ,χ(μχ)=πe2τvzαJ2JT3×64μχ2JΓ(2-1J)Γ(92-1J)fbc(J)BχxBχy,ℓxym,χ(μχ)=πe2τvzαJ2JT3×64μχ2JΓ(2-1J)Γ(92-1J)fm(J)BχxBχy.

Wiedemann-Franz law

From the explicit expressions of the in-plane longitudinal and transverse components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^\chi$$\end{document}σχ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^\chi$$\end{document}ℓχ, which we have derived [cf. Eqs. (31), (34), (61), and (62)], we immediately find that the relation64\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sigma ^{\chi } _{ij} = \frac{ 3 \,e^2 }{\pi ^2\, T} \, \ell ^{ \chi } _{ij} + \mathcal {O}(\beta ^{-2}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σijχ=3e2π2Tℓijχ+O(β-2)is satisfied. This is equivalent to satisfying the Wiedemann-Franz law, which holds in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}β→∞57. Hence, we have demonstrated that the Wiedemann-Franz law continues to hold even in the presence of OMM. This relation tells us that, knowing the nature of the magnetoelectric conductivity, we can infer the behaviour of the magnetothermal coefficient. Therefore, it is not necessary to provide any separate plots for this response.

Summary and future perspectives

In this paper, we have considered planar Hall (or planar thermal Hall) configurations such that a 3d Weyl or multi-Weyl semimetal is subjected to a conjunction of an electric field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E (and/or temperature gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _{{\textbf{r}} } T$$\end{document}∇rT) and an effective magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ, oriented at a generic angle with respect to each other. The z-axis is chosen to be along the direction along which the mWSM shows a linear-in-momentum dispersion, and is perpendicular to the plane of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{E}}$$\end{document}E (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _{{\textbf{r}} } T$$\end{document}∇rT) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ. The effective magnetic field consists of two parts — (a) an actual/physical magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}$$\end{document}B, and (b) an emergent magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 which arises if the sample is subjected to elastic deformations (strain tensor field). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 exhibits a chiral nature, because it couples to conjugate nodal points of opposite chiralities with opposite signs, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}} + \chi \, {\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B+χB5. The relative orientations of these two constituents of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{B}}}_\chi$$\end{document}Bχ, with respect to the direction of the electric field (or temperature gradient), give rise to a rich variety of possibilities in the characteristics of the electric, thermal, and thermoelectric response tensors. We have derived explicit expressions for these response coefficients, which have helped us to identify unambiguously the interplay of the BC- and OMM-contributions. In addition, we have illustrated the overall behaviour of the response in some realistic parameter regimes. We have found that the total (i.e., sum) and the axial (i.e., difference) combinations of the response, from the two conjugate nodes, depend strongly on the specific value of J. In particular, for the planar transverse components of the response tensors, while the OMM part acts exclusively in opposition with the BC-only part for the Weyl semimetals, the former syncs with the latter for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J>1$$\end{document}J>1, thereby enhancing the overall response. The strain-induced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5 provides a way to have linear-in-B terms in the response coefficients for untilted nodes, in addition to the quadratic-in-B dependence.

The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^2$$\end{document}B2-dependence and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi$$\end{document}π-periodic behaviour (with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}θ) of the magnetoelectric conductivity in the absence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_5$$\end{document}B5 have been observed in numerous experiments, which involve materials like ZrTe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_{5}$$\end{document}560, TaAs61, NbP and NbAs17, and Co\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}3Sn\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}2S\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}262, known to host Weyl nodes. Furthermore, the magnetothermal coefficient has also been measured in materials such as NdAlSi63, which again shows the expected \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^2$$\end{document}B2-dependence. It is possible to modify these experimental set-ups to devise the mechanism for applying strain gradients to the samples, thus leading to the realization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_5$$\end{document}B564 in addition.

For the case of a negative chemical potential, we need to focus on the valence band at the corresponding node. The energy, band velocity, and Berry curvature will have opposite signs compared to the case of the positive chemical potential. For the magnetic-field-independent “Drude” parts, we have found that, independent of the J-values, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )$$\end{document}σxx0,χ(μχ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi )$$\end{document}ℓxx0,χ(μχ) are proportional to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi ^2$$\end{document}μχ2, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^{0, \chi }_{xx} (\mu _\chi ) \propto \mu _\chi$$\end{document}αxx0,χ(μχ)∝μχ. For the magnetic-field-dependent parts, the leading-order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi$$\end{document}μχ-dependence goes as (i) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi ^{ -\frac{2}{J} }$$\end{document}μχ-2J for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^{\chi }_{ij}$$\end{document}σijχ, (ii) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi ^{ -1- \frac{2}{J} }$$\end{document}μχ-1-2J for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^{\chi }_{ij}$$\end{document}αijχ, and (iii) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{ \frac{2}{J} }$$\end{document}μ2J for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{\chi }_{ij}$$\end{document}ℓijχ. Hence, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=3$$\end{document}J=3, the non-Drude parts depend on a fractional power of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi$$\end{document}μχ, which physically makes no sense for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi < 0$$\end{document}μχ<0. Now we must remember that, in our formalism, we have not included \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi$$\end{document}μχ in the starting Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}_\chi$$\end{document}Hχ, but have included it in the Fermi distribution function. This has allowed us to derive analytical expressions by applying the Sommerfeld expansion, and the form of the final expressions (summarized above) are an artifact of our specific procedure. Therefore, we conclude that it is possible to derive the results for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi < 0$$\end{document}μχ<0 by implementing the same procedure, but considering the transport contributed by a valence band (with the sign changes quoted above), for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=1, 2$$\end{document}J=1,2. However, it will not work for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=3$$\end{document}J=3 due to the presence of fractional powers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi$$\end{document}μχ. The results for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=1,2$$\end{document}J=1,2 are thus expected to have a dependence on the various parameters similar to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\chi >0$$\end{document}μχ>0 case that we have considered here, modulo possible minus signs for the various contributing parts. As for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=3$$\end{document}J=3 case, we can include the negative chemical potential at the Hamiltonian level, such that it directly enters into the energy eigenvalue expressions, and then numerically evaluate the behaviour of the various response tensors.

In Refs.27,28, the authors have included a momentum-independent internode scattering time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document}τv, in addition to the intranode scattering time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}τ. The inclusion of the internode processes results in stabilizing the chiral anomaly, as it inherently leads to different chemical potentials in the two conjugate WSM/mWSM nodes. By plugging in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document}τv as a phenomenological constant (because of ignoring its momentum dependence), Ref.27 has considered the resulting semiclassical Boltzmann equations for the two nodes in an untilted WSM. The authors have shown that a finite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v$$\end{document}τv is tied to a difference in the chemical potential between the two nodes, given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \mu \propto \left( {\textbf{E}} \cdot {\textbf{B}} \right) \tau _v$$\end{document}Δμ∝E·Bτv (in the absence of a pseudomagnetic field). They have inferred that this results in the magnetoelectric conductivity tensor components acquiring extra contributions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\propto \tau _v$$\end{document}∝τv and normal physical conditions dictate that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _v \gg \tau$$\end{document}τv≫τ. The same behaviour is found for the case of the thermoelectric conductivity tensor. A more complete treatment can be found in36, where the authors do not assume a momentum-independent internode-scattering time, and they go beyond the relaxation-time approximation. It remains to be seen how the above calculations pan out for the mWSMs and multifold semimetals65, and what are the forms of the resulting final expressions.

In the future, it will be worthwhile to perform the same calculations by including tilted nodes27,28,36,39, because tilting is applicable for generic scenarios. A tilt can induce terms which are linearly-dependent on B, even in the absence of a strain-induced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf{B}}_5$$\end{document}B5-part. Furthermore, a more realistic calculational set-up should include internode scatterings28 and going beyond the relaxation-time approximation36. Although we have considered the weak-magnetic-field scenario in this paper, under the influence of a strong quantizing magnetic field, we have to incorporate the effects of the discrete Landau levels while computing the linear response33,66–68. Other auxiliary directions include the study of linear and nonlinear response in the presence of disorder and/or strong correlations69–76. One could also explore the effects of a time-periodic drive21,77,78, for instance, by shining circularly polarized light.
